LLM supporting:  pendred syndrome is characterized by a specific combination of hormone abnormalities, primarily affecting the thyroid gland and, in some cases, the adrenal glands. the key hormonal features include : 1. * * thyroid hormone deficiency * * : patients with pendred syndrome have a significant reduction in the production of thyroid hormones ( thyroxine, t4, and triiodothyronine, t3 ). this is due to a congenital defect in the thyroid gland that impairs the synthesis and release of these hormones. 2. * * elevated thyroid - stimulating hormone ( tsh ) * * : as a result of the thyroid hormone deficiency, there is an elevation in the levels of tsh, which is secreted by the pituitary gland to stimulate the thyroid gland. 3. * * congenital adrenal hyperplasia ( cah ) * * : some patients with pendred syndrome also present with cah, which is associated with a deficiency in cortisol and aldosterone. this can lead to an increase in androgen production, resulting in precocious virilization and growth retardation. 4. * * impaired iodine organification * * : a characteristic feature of pendred syndrome is a defect in the organification of iodine, which is essential for the synthesis of thyroid hormones. this defect leads to an inability to convert iodine into an active form that can be used in the iodination of tyrosine residues in thyroglobulin. 5. * * reduced t3 production * * : in addition to the reduced t4, patients with pendred syndrome also show a decrease in t3, the more metabolically active form of thyroid hormone. 6. * * normal t4 to t3 ratio * * : unlike in other forms of hypothyroidism, the ratio of t4 to t3 remains normal in pendred syndrome, indicating a specific defect in the t3 pathway. these hormonal abnormalities collectively contribute to the clinical manifestations of pendred syndrome, including goiter, hypothyroidism, and, in some cases, adrenal dysfunction.

=== idx: 617 ===
Gold supporting: in addition to the recognition and excision of dna damage throughout the genome ( ggr ), there exists a mechanism, transcription - coupled nucleotide excision repair ( tcr ), for recognizing some types of dna damage in the transcribed strand of genes in escherichia coli, yeast and mammalian cells.
LLM supporting:  transcription - coupled repair ( tcr ) primarily targets the transcribed strand of actively transcribed genes. this is because the tcr mechanism is designed to address the specific challenges posed by dna damage in regions where transcription is ongoing. the rna polymerase, which is essential for transcribing the dna into mrna, can be stalled at sites of dna damage, thereby impeding the normal flow of genetic information. tcr facilitates the removal of these lesions from the transcribed strand, allowing the rna polymerase to resume its function and thus restoring the integrity of the transcribed dna. while the provided context mentions the involvement of rad26 in the repair of both the transcribed and non - transcribed strands, the primary focus of tcr is on the transcribed strand due to its critical role in gene expression.
